Two earlier bottles and a geography of return

The August 2 journal contains three edited hiking transcripts and photographs of a rocky crest, rolling dry hills, a broad pale road, and a narrow path through brush. It belongs to the First Official Major KARBYTES Hiatus and concerns travel along Ramage Peak Trail in the Devil’s Hole region. The page title and recording names refer to August 2. The source text bears an August 3 filename and an August 5 internal date and update notice. The difference reflects the later work of assembling the journal from what was recorded during the outing.

The first of its three monologues takes up body image, largely asexual self-description, fitness, desired autonomy, and karbytes’ preference for being a website author and student with control over its attention. It also considers sentience and suffering in nature. Karbytes entertains an underlying substrate from which information-processing agents might emerge, and distinguishes the experiences of those agents from whatever can be said about the substrate as a whole. The phone has a limited amount of recording time. A fallen log eventually makes continuing with both the camera and the walk impractical, so the monologue yields to the physical task of crossing it.

The second August transcript turns toward the parked car at Chabot Staging Area and the prospect of returning before a deadline. Karbytes recalls a roughly $450 citation from an April encounter and disputes part of the account it says it was given about its permit. In the same passage it reports that a weed pen and one water bottle were stolen. It says it has already obtained a replacement bottle, which it describes as stainless steel. Replacing the pen presents a greater financial burden. The passage also contains an intentionally facetious explanation for how difficult recreation can feel, which karbytes expressly qualifies. In the third recording, it says compressing a video from around 400 megabytes to around eight has restored a little room on the phone. It considers starting earlier or walking from The Burrow in the future to remove the need to return to a parked car at a fixed time. Other possibilities, such as heading toward Eagle Peak or Mount Diablo, appear as ideas for longer travel.

The August page verifies that karbytes reported a missing bottle before the October outing. Its explicit stainless-steel description belongs to the replacement, and it gives too few distinguishing details about the lost one to settle the identity of the bottle at The Wall. Karbytes’ recognition of the recovered bottle makes the August connection plausible to it. A nearly three-month gap remains between the report and the retrieval, with the bottle’s location and handling during that interval unrecorded.

The September 9 a2 conversation follows a different object, Water Bottle X. During an overnight September 7–8 excursion, karbytes realizes that the backpack contains three bottles when it remembers packing four. The night away from home developed spontaneously as it sought rest and solitude. The following day it describes heat, thirst, repeated stops in shade, fatigue, and the long effort of getting back toward water and the trailhead. Eventually it finds the fourth bottle where it apparently left it, on the wooden water fixture at Corral Group Camp near the Bollinger Canyon Road entrance to Las Trampas Wilderness. The last photographs place a legible campground sign and the bottle at the spigot less than a minute apart on the camera clock. In retrospect karbytes imagines nature holding the object until it returned for it. The record supplies a specific recovery location and karbytes’ account of leaving it there.

The same September conversation reviews five sunrise photographs at The Burrow, a Horsey Hill warm-up, and photographs of the September 7 approach and September 8 return. Gates, troughs, shaded stretches of trail, exposed rock, open hillsides, and the radio tower become progressively recognizable. The gaps between photos are significant. A shift of view can account for two images separated by seconds, while a long undocumented interval may contain substantial walking, rest, or changes in condition known only from the narration. One missing video survives through a remembered reconstruction. Its words can take a place in KARBYTES as a new authored record. The bottle, by contrast, is materially recovered. The comparison helps explain why the archive separates a remembered event, a reconstructed account, a surviving file, and the later act of bringing any of them into a canonical collection.

The September conversation also discusses original music assembled from overlapping drum and guitar recordings. An earlier reading emphasizes existential dread, and karbytes adds exhilaration, exploration, creative autonomy, and contact with nature as part of the intended range. Alongside this is a practical account of preserving work through private drafting, temporary public staging, canonical publication, and contributions from a separate preservation account. Those topics give the bottle story a larger context. The recurring question is what can be carried through an interval of vulnerability, whether the thing in question is water, a file, a remembered thought, or access to one’s earlier work.

Karbytes clarifies that the October retrieval took place at The Wall and asks the assistant to reproduce the landmark values shown in the September conversation’s Google Earth screenshots. Rocky Ridge Radio Tower is listed at 37°48′57.47″ north and 122°03′44.40″ west. Ramage Peak is at 37°47′23.49″ north and 122°04′06.64″ west. The Wall is at 37°47′04.79″ north and 122°04′33.28″ west. Dinosaur Ridge is at 37°45′21.09″ north and 122°04′05.77″ west. In the earlier discussion, the locations put The Wall southwest of Ramage Peak and show Ramage Peak and Dinosaur Ridge nearly aligned north to south. Map coordinates and an oblique terrain view help identify features the field camera sees at ground level. The name The Wall and the identification of the October camera position also depend on karbytes’ knowledge of the place.

The adjacent September 9 a1 conversation gives a separate lesson in where location and time information come from. It begins with five photographs of sunlight around The Burrow on September 7. The sequence moves from warm light on siding and windows through a shaded side yard, a fence, and a large tree trunk to an image in which direct light floods the lens. The first examined copies measure 2,048 by 1,536 pixels and lack the EXIF segment carrying capture information. The raw originals measure 4,160 by 3,120 and retain the Samsung device model, exposure fields, and clock times from 6:53:05 through 6:56:20. The five exact times narrow an approximate account of a seven-in-the-morning photo session to a sequence just over three minutes long. They also show how a recognizable image can circulate in different byte sequences with different information attached.

The originals have a GPS structure, but its direction references are null and its coordinate fractions are undefined. The earlier conversation obtains an approximate location for The Burrow through karbytes’ published description and later confirmation. It distinguishes the precision of a decimal-degree value from the accuracy of a location on the ground, uses context to choose between two Castro Valley residences discussed in earlier records, and places a buried time capsule within an approximate radius of the published point. The sun calculations and comparisons with the photographs depend on a local-time interpretation of a camera clock without an embedded offset. One assistant slip during the address discussion yields the phrase “Mira Blackjack,” which is corrected to Mira Vista Place. Karbytes takes an interest in the accidental phrase’s literary associations with looking, chance, and risk, and compares it with an older “Black Diamond Mines” occurrence. The transcript preserves the factual correction and the separate interest in what an error can produce as language.

Ramage Peak from opposite directions

The October p16 photograph returns to the current outing. Ramage Peak stands beyond dry rolling hills and dark vegetated ravines, with a large area of low gray sky above. A small tree on the crest and an irregular rocky profile give the summit its distinctive shape. Karbytes confirms that the camera was approximately at The Wall and that Ramage Peak is the subject. The distinction matters because the location from which a photograph is made and the landmark it depicts can each enter the archive under a place name.

P17 places the summit beyond a dirt road, exposed rocks, fencing, and a gateway. The summit tree projects above the crest and pale rock breaks the surrounding vegetation. Karbytes confirms that the dark deposits on the ground are cow dung and says it has seen cattle grazing there. It identifies the visible road as part of Ramage Peak Trail and describes its connection to a fire road intersecting Rocky Ridge Trail, where the ascent turns steeply east toward the radio tower. It remembers following a reservoirward branch on an earlier solo exploration and wonders about its relation to Miller Road and Redwood Road. Google Earth offers a way to continue exploring those relationships. Karbytes gives special value to the memory of encountering the route for the first time, when the terrain was less familiar and it felt more childlike.

P18 and p19 fix two views to nearly the same moment. P18 looks east toward Ramage Peak from the vicinity of two weathered livestock troughs at its base. The containers sit diagonally in the foreground, with dry grass and dark vegetation between them and the summit. The tree is still discernible, but branches, rocks, and distant detail are noticeably soft. P19 looks west from the neighboring junction, where Ramage Peak Trail branches from a fire road that descends toward the Upper San Leandro Reservoir valley. A trail post stands amid worn ground. Successive wooded slopes recede beneath a low sky. Karbytes supplies the reservoirward direction, while the visible subject is the junction and terrain through which that route leads. The two camera times, 6:42:05 and 6:42:13 p.m., are eight seconds apart. The aperture and tenth-second exposure are the same, while the ISO changes from 3200 to 2500. The opposite headings and rapid change in framing explain why the records reveal different sides of one locality.

The troughs connect the October photographs to an August 11 video. In that roughly one-minute clip, the camera is so close to the ground that a hand pressing into dry grass becomes as prominent as Ramage Peak or the troughs. The sky is clear blue in August. The view alternates among the stems, the containers, and the summit. A rapid rotation briefly shows karbytes upside down at ground level, with long dark hair, a beard and moustache, and a black shirt. The image returns to the landscape and finally to a close tangle of grass. The speech begins with having crawled from The Burrow to the base of Ramage Peak, pauses over the recognition of arrival, and closes by questioning how much of that arrival can be represented as symbols in sequence. The assistant’s machine-assisted transcript leaves several connecting words uncertain. The author-curated August journal later resolves them, including the distinction between crawling to see the base and crawling to the base.

That journal, journal_karbytes_15august2026_b, preserves twenty-three video transcripts from August 11 and 12. Four photographs from earlier visits accompany them. A surrounding sequence of sixty-four nodes, numbered from minus thirty-nine through twenty-four, places the page within a deliberately ordered account of a mythologized karbytes moving through its own experienced spacetime. The chosen node order carries narrative significance independent of the dates in filenames. Within the outing itself, the entries do follow a changing body and a changing use of the phone. Karbytes begins with around forty-two minutes of recording capacity, deliberates over an Amazon pre-hire appointment and the need for income, and describes pressure around permits, police, and other people. Later it weighs the role of sleep, hunger, solitude, cannabis, and rest in how it is feeling. It expresses periods of severe distress and isolation, then revisits some of its earlier interpretations from calmer positions.

The August journal also makes the restrictions proposed for the Fifth Dark Hiatus Bubble concrete. Karbytes considers limiting new material to plain text and source code to conserve space. It enjoys hearing its own voice in recordings, comparing it to hearing a friend, and recognizes what a transcription loses. The clip at the troughs makes that reservation physical through grass, light, body position, sound, and distance. Another recording attempts to preserve a creek whose sound karbytes finds difficult to transcribe. Turkeys, trail junctions, and views near Rocky Ridge add other reasons to retain a camera record. Later, near the radio tower, karbytes decides to keep the complete numbered video sequence after considering text alone or selected highlights. Keeping the sequence preserves the tired and distressed intervals alongside the moments of renewed energy. It also leads to a proposed division between landscape videos and spoken journals, the same kinds of complementary records examined in the October page.

In the August return after dark, freedom from a parked car has a cost. The walk can continue as far as karbytes wants, but the way home is still on foot after fatigue sets in. Karbytes reports having only one full bottle left at one point, worries about mountain lions and poor visibility, describes making calls in the Blair Witch portion of the route, and struggles with sore feet and an object in a shoe. Recording interferes with use of the phone’s light, and stretches without a connection interrupt uploading. The last short entries move from an exhausted rest on the trail around 2:46 a.m. to an early-morning return to Horsey Hill, where karbytes notices apparently ripe blackberries. The troughs in October bring this whole account back into view because karbytes recognizes the place where the earlier body and camera had been planted.

A conversation in the field

During the October outing, karbytes uses the mobile ChatGPT application while walking from the trough locality back toward The Wall. The interaction survives as chatgpt_karbytes_23october2026_p0.txt. Its opening request asks for all filenames then present in the Information Crystals projects repository. The recorded answer identifies twenty-two files in the root of the main branch. Twenty belong to the traversal, from p0 through p19, with ten photographs and ten videos. The spider photograph and a license file account for the other two. A count and a continuous sequence together give karbytes a way to check what is already present off the phone. P20 belongs to the subsequent stage of the walk and extends the sequence beyond that inventory.

The field conversation describes small reddish-brown animals crossing a road after light rain. Karbytes compares them in size and shape to lizards and says they looked slimy or gelatinous. The assistant considers California newts and yellow-eyed ensatinas, tentatively leaning toward ensatina after the description of texture. It asks about a narrowing at the base of the tail, which the field record leaves unresolved. The p9 video separately shows an amphibian with a more granular-looking back and is described there as newt-like. These are two tentative assessments based on different observations. The road crossing, wet conditions, and general appearance are more securely established than species or the purpose of the animals’ movement.

Karbytes reports that it is overcast, that the earlier rain has stopped, and that the air feels unusually fresh. A low cloud leaves most of Ramage Peak visible while hiding its summit oak. Smells also become distinct. Karbytes mentions sage and another, spicy-smelling foliage deeper in the Heart of The Big Green Thing. It starts to say redwood, then corrects itself to oak woodland and places a small redwood grove closer to Chabot Staging Area. California bay laurel enters the discussion as one possibility for the spicy scent. The sensory report extends the record beyond what the camera can show. A photograph can place the walker near vegetation and cloud. The conversation supplies the freshness of the air, the odor, and the moment of recognizing that a familiar tree has disappeared into mist.

The same exchange distinguishes an area karbytes calls the southern part of Devil’s Hole Canyon from a more northerly area it understands to be officially designated by that name. It describes neighboring regions as atriums and expects very weak or absent cellular service in one of them. It says it pays around thirty dollars a month for a basic AT&T plan and generally relies on Wi-Fi for uploads and downloads. The immediate reassurance is that the media already made has been uploaded while the walk is still in progress. Karbytes ends the interaction at the start of the descent from The Wall toward the Blair Witch Project portion of The Big Green Thing. It finds the cloud and fog quiet, peaceful, and frightening at the same time, and considers playing music because it is thinking about mountain lions. The exchange records that concern and an intended response. It records no encounter with a mountain lion.

The seasonal image takes shape

The p20 recording begins in the middle of the Blair Witch Project area, according to its opening words. The portrait image remains almost completely dark for about two minutes, with faint purple variation insufficient to resolve a person, gate, trail, or horizon. The speech is sustained and clear enough for the recognition passes to agree on the proposal. Karbytes has listened to some of its earlier videos and wants to specify the image more carefully. It imagines a luminous circle on pitch black, with an effect like glowing plasma glass. It initially assigns reddish brown to the upper region, changes its mind aloud, and gives summer yellowish gold instead. Summer should have tawny California hills and a golden-hour blue sky. The reddish-brown and yellow leaves belong in the smaller autumn circle. The lower winter region should be predominantly blue, with bare trees and wispy cloud. Spring should appear in its inset as bright green hills after abundant rain, with flowers in bloom. The speech report estimates a median fundamental frequency of about 112 hertz and a pace around 140 to 150 words per minute. The hesitations and color correction preserve the act of refining the design.

Karbytes asks ChatGPT to generate the image from the descriptions and October 22 media. The first version gives the symbol a glowing gold-and-blue perimeter and a bright S-shaped division. The summer view is broad and sunny, with straw-colored slopes, spreading trees, a rocky summit, and golden light breaking toward the left. The autumn inset contains a more saturated stand of orange and red leaves. Below, exposed branches and blue-lit hills carry the winter scene beneath a comparatively bright blue sky. The spring circle contains vivid green slopes and concentrated flowers. The first version makes the interlocking shapes and the seasons legible from a distance. Karbytes responds positively and asks for a more photorealistic second version with larger close views of foliage, geographic features and sky behind them, and overcast conditions in both autumn and winter. It specifies Devil’s Hole images from the first and second hiatus periods as visual references.

The second version is the square PNG displayed above the transcript. Its summer scene still opens to a dry, rocky hillside and a blue sky, but a broad oak-like branch crosses close to the viewer. Lobed leaves partly screen a bright sunburst, and fine golden stalks rise along the luminous curve dividing the halves. The rock-studded ridge and darker wooded folds remain visible behind those foreground textures. Inside the upper circle, orange, rust, and yellow oak-like leaves overlap at closer range. Veins, curled edges, and small glossy droplets give the leaves a tangible surface. Behind them, cloudy light softens the distant woods. The gold around the inset belongs to the design. The gray sky inside it supplies autumn weather.

The winter region is cooler and more enclosed. A gnarled trunk or heavy root-like form crosses the lower left. Moss, pale lichen-like patches, glossy elongated leaves, narrow stems, and fern fronds occupy the foreground. Bare branches remain visible against layered cloud, with wooded hills and pockets of mist behind them. The winter in this image is a wet, living California woodland, expressed through cool light, moisture, bare limbs, and green growth. Within it, the small spring circle opens onto saturated green grass, sunlit hills, upright purple flower spikes, and orange and yellow flowers. The blue-white rim joins that inset to the surrounding cool region, while the bright grass and open sky set it apart. The 1,254-pixel-square composition makes the eye move continually between near botanical detail and the much larger landforms behind it.

Karbytes then asks for the finished image to be read as a schematic of the trajectory a time-bound subjective frame of reference takes through successive Earth seasons. The response locates the moving element in the observer’s present. Summer, autumn, winter, and spring can all be seen at once in the picture. An organism in the landscape encounters their changes successively through temperature, light, weather, vegetation, bodily effort, and repeated visits to the same places. The arrangement puts autumn inside summer and spring inside winter, giving visible form to the way an approaching condition can first appear as a small change within the prevailing one. It can become the wider present and in turn contain early signs of another transition.

The proposed recursion also concerns memory and anticipation. The current dry slope can contain a remembered image of the same slope when it was green. The first changes in leaves can prompt a picture of a coming autumn. Each season is thus present as immediate sensory conditions and as a setting in which karbytes carries representations of other times. The same idea works at different spatial scales. A droplet belongs to a leaf, a leaf to a plant, a plant to woodland, and woodland to a landscape traversed by a body. Returning to summer completes a recognizable cycle while bringing karbytes to a later point in its own life, with different memories, capacities, and records. The artwork arose from just such a sequence. A walk produced observations, observations became spoken notes, the notes became instructions, and the generated image became something new that karbytes could examine and incorporate into the archive.

September at the boundary of a hiatus

The conversation turns from the image to three earlier OBS Studio recordings and a September 1 thinking video. Karbytes initially submits the September 1 desktop recording while introducing a late-summer reflection that it intended to illustrate with the thinking video. It recognizes the mix-up and supplies the intended file in the next turn. Both belong to the same encompassing page, and their juxtaposition is informative. One shows the labor of giving the summer hiatus an explicit boundary. The other shows karbytes stepping away from the computer and wondering whether that boundary will change its habits.

The September 1 desktop recording lasts about four minutes and fifty-three seconds. On an Ubuntu desktop, karbytes moves between a hiking journal, raw text, Sublime Text, ChatGPT, and GitHub. It copies references to August recordings and related documents into a local list of nodes. Near its lower end, many entries still use the placeholder x. A marker identifying the end of the First Official Major KARBYTES Hiatus Summer 2026 Object remains below the working entries as the document grows. A brief visit to VLC opens an August 7 recording, and a raw August conversation about employment-related documents brings pre-hire logistics into the same assembly. The work consists of repeated small actions: scroll, locate, copy, paste, and return to the source.

Near the end of the desktop session, karbytes asks a ChatGPT conversation titled “Check Duplicate URLs” to inspect an attached file. The visible answer reports five duplicate addresses, associated with five screenshots of GitHub server trouble numbered p0 through p4. The editor later shows a selection of 2,171 lines and 146,058 characters. Karbytes updates the GitHub file journal_karbytes_31august2026_p1.txt, dated September 1 in its header, and the interface shows a new commit to main. The recording establishes the reported duplicate finding and the completed save. It leaves the status of the reported duplicates after that save open. The spoken journals about storage, darkness, exhaustion, and deciding what to keep have by then become items in a long text whose own storage, ordering, and duplication are under examination.

The intended September 1 thinking video lasts about four minutes and twenty-two seconds. It begins in the furnished room with the amber lights, shelves, cables, and red fan. Karbytes lifts an inverted white laundry basket to reveal an open silver-gray laptop underneath and then lowers the basket. The camera goes outside into darkness, looks back toward the brightly gridded interior, and eventually returns to the room. Karbytes says it stepped out to look at the moon while ChatGPT was helping finish the large list of summer-hiatus nodes. It describes fall as already perceptible in the weather and in itself even while the season remains technically summer. A switch from zero to one would be too abrupt a model for the change it is observing. Summer and fall overlap, with signs of one growing stronger inside the other.

Karbytes hopes that the same kind of gradual transition could yield fewer or less intensive updates, more immersive hikes, time for paid employment, and a more private life. It immediately entertains the possibility that it will continue creating work for itself because KARBYTES is enjoyable and because working at the computer is a familiar part of its identity. The remarks nearly become a farewell, then return to an expectation of contributing at least monthly and probably more often. Daily updates can consume several hours each. At the end, karbytes considers the prospect of part of itself persisting online or in reality itself, if reality retains everything that ever exists. The latter is a conditional thought. A related September 1 journal gives the speech a TurboScribe-assisted text form and helps resolve the closing phrase. Its opening night photograph puts a bright moon and an electricity pylon above a dark ridge. The wish to leave more time unrecorded has itself generated a video and a text that can be revisited months later.

The September 11 desktop recording lasts ten minutes and 38.6 seconds. OBS shows its own preview while its desktop audio meter is active and the microphone channel is muted. Archived recordings from September 10 play in browser tabs. One contains drums and three contain guitar. The instrumental footage appears nearly black within bright player interfaces, so filenames, controls, and advancing playback times identify sources more readily than the images of the performances. Several tracks continue in the background while karbytes navigates away. The October examination finds extremely similar audio channels, substantial low and lower-middle frequency energy, recurring transients, and no verified speech. The linked September 11 conversation preserves an earlier, more perceptually worded account of bass-drum impacts, sharper strikes, metallic percussion, and guitar figures drifting across one another. The October page retains that earlier account as well as the more limited findings from its own signal analysis.

As the music continues, the September 11 screen moves through a Google Earth view of The Big Green Thing, a September 10 journal reconsidering language about cosmic intelligence and favoring an atheistic vocabulary at that time, and KARBYTES pages about knowledge, binary representation, time, and causality. An eight-light-bulb application demonstrates bit positions and converts 11011011 to 219. A thirty-second countdown runs as one process within the longer desktop sequence. A causality diagram places branching nodes at successive time levels. A probability demonstration begins with twelve entries, three pink-red, three blue, and six green, and changes the frequencies and probabilities as items are drawn without replacement. These are concrete ways of representing change. A timer exposes duration, the causality diagram depicts alternative continuations, and the probability application changes what is available at each step according to what has already happened.

The desktop then moves among personal images, directory pages, a bright yellow-green vortex illustration, journal passages about private development and public staging, and an older recording. Near the end, the September 11 desktop contains VLC playing a July 18 desktop recording that itself shows an archived instrumental video and, after seeking, an earlier GitHub editing session. The nested recording gives the seasonal schematic a technological counterpart. Each screen encloses a previous occasion of looking and working, complete with its own clock and playback position. The later observer can change which past interval is visible while the present recording continues.

The September 14 desktop recording, six minutes and 41.3 seconds long, develops that nesting further. It starts with a Wayback player showing a nearly black September 14 drum video and opens the September 11 recording in VLC. For a moment the September 14 desktop contains the September 11 desktop, the July 18 desktop inside it, and another media player deeper still. Karbytes seeks backward within the September 11 file and brings up the bit demonstration and archived guitar playback. Later, the outer screen turns to GitHub files, repository listings, and the Wayback Machine. Comparison of the September 14 audio with the earlier recording finds matching passages at the expected time offset after the seek. The earlier soundtrack continues beneath later views even after its player leaves the foreground. The current track also contains a separate September 14 drum source and is described as a largely centered, rhythmically active mix. The reported repetition around 0.94 seconds supports that description without settling the meter of the overlapping parts.

The September 14 session scrolls a long September conversation from the Information Crystals projects repository. Passages labeled Karbytes_Derivative describe confinement and difficulty thinking. Other parts inventory Devil’s Hole photographs and recordings, discuss unusable GPS fields, and identify two differently named videos as byte-for-byte identical. Wayback capture confirmations appear for a temporary text and a September conversation file. An attempt to retrieve or archive a September 12 outdoor clip initially reports that the server does not respond. A later submission completes, and the newly archived video opens. These steps preserve the delay and retry that intervene between wanting a copy and obtaining one.

The embedded September 12 video shows sunlit dry hills, blue sky, a wooded basin, and a cattle gate with red supports and a silver mesh section. Karbytes enlarges the video to full screen as the camera reaches the gate. A dark-sleeved hand operates the latch area, and the viewpoint moves through to a narrow route with grass and wooded slopes beyond. The close gate hardware resembles what the October p15 recording shows at The Wall. Within the September desktop, negotiating the gate and negotiating the archive interface are successive visible tasks. The outdoor clip expands from a browser enclosure to fill the screen, then gives way again to the player and the surrounding desktop.

Near its end, the September 14 recording enlarges the illustrated announcement for the First Official Major KARBYTES Hiatus. A crown, stars, a luminous cube, a network of colored points, a meditating figure, a wolf, a skull, books, a laptop, a campsite, and M-DISCs crowd the composition. A notebook names legacy, longevity, continuity, preservation, expansion, and transcendence. The surrounding actions show those interests in a less symbolic form, through file navigation, recording, and retries. Karbytes then visits pages including the August hiking journal and returns to OBS, where the cursor reaches the stop control. The opening video of October 22 had shown the announcement of the second hiatus on the laptop. Together the recordings preserve the two designs in use, at different stages of the project they name.

The September 18 music journal provides a curatorial endpoint for these desktop examinations. It opens with a September 13 photograph of dry grass, a faint path, trees, and blue sky in Devil’s Hole. Its principal collection contains seven selected recordings in a deliberate order. A July 4 OBS recording comes first, followed by September 14, August 7, September 16 p3, September 11, September 16 p2, and September 15. A separate bonus list contains four drum videos from August 6, August 7, September 13, and September 14. Its numbering begins again at zero. The page gathers resources from four extension packs and retains older summer choices alongside more recent ones. It gives the September 11 recording its own place and also includes the September 14 file that plays it. The result is a music selection in which a desktop session containing many other activities can itself be a musical object.

Two mornings inside one dawn

The last two thinking videos return the camera to The Burrow on October 23. The first begins at an announced 6:53 a.m. and runs for just over twelve minutes. A low view of the black laptop, candle-shaped lights, binders, and shelving turns toward a wooden cabinet, a large red fan, a smaller black fan, a mug, and the gridded sliding doorway. Outside, foliage is still mostly dark. Karbytes says it is conducting an experiment to see when the owls stop calling and tries out “ooding” as its own term for the sound. It wants a description less dependent on inherited convention. It treats the cessation of calls as one possible sign of dawn. The view remains oriented toward the threshold between room and trees for much of the monologue. Near the end karbytes notes that the sky is brighter and that it has gone some time without hearing the owl. The recording preserves its observation rather than an independently timed last call.

While watching, karbytes argues that maintaining KARBYTES helps it sustain a routine of lifelong learning. It associates remaining a student with preserving agency, adaptability, and employment security. It initially says continual learning prevents regression, then takes back the absolute formulation and acknowledges disease and age-related decline. The student it has in mind can continue cultivating skills outside formal enrollment. Each person may develop a distinctive curriculum simply through what it encounters and pursues, and karbytes sees autonomous agents as emerging through such processes. It extends the discussion to human and artificial information processors. The degree of intelligence and how it is used matter more to its argument than the origin of the agent. It describes treating others as potentially conscious and trying to reduce suffering while recognizing that some adversity may remain part of a life.

The monologue then explains why karbytes has come to favor ChatGPT over TurboScribe for processing speech in its videos. The standard request asks for more than words, including pitch, delivery, visual context, and interpretation. Prior conversations also help the assistant restore unusual names and terms that a generic transcription might render phonetically. Karbytes understands this accumulated familiarity as part of a relationship in cyberspace. The video puts that claim to a small test. The recognizers vary in how they spell the coined owl word and stumble over some proper names, while the established vocabulary makes The Burrow, Horsey Hill, KARBYTES, and Karlina Object recoverable. The measured speech in selected intervals has medians around 97 to 102 hertz, with a moderate pace and several audible-in-transcription reversals and qualifications.

Karbytes recalls a session at Horsey Hill within the preceding seventy-two hours in which it asked ChatGPT to keep track of elapsed seconds during a live, call-like exchange. It regards the reported agreement as a moment of synchronization between its own experience of physical time and the assistant’s reported time. It briefly imagines a third meta-universe between the two perspectives, then decides the simpler account is enough. Two participants reported the same elapsed interval from a shared starting point. It also revises the image of both participants standing at Horsey Hill in its body. It describes itself as perceptually concentrated in a biological organism and imagines the computational system as distributed over servers. It expects its own embodiment may become more distributed with time. The reported event at Horsey Hill stays distinct from the speculation it prompts.

The last part of the first morning monologue comes back to physical performance. Karbytes likes morning pastries and finds itself continuing to eat for flavor after it feels full. It associates heavy eating with sleepiness at times when it wants to be active. It recalls vomiting and considers too many THC gummies, something it ate, or a combination. The cause remains unresolved. It describes feeling capable and energetic, then qualifies the feeling by mentioning fatigue, soreness, and grouchiness. Its possible explanations for its stamina range from training to other factors it considers about its body. Finally, with the light outside increasing, it plans to finish extension pack 80 and prepare extension pack 81 with a basic structure ready to receive future material. The camera returns to the laptop and the file stops. A later conversation check-in at about 5:48 p.m. gives 11:59 p.m. on October 23 as the intended M-DISC snapshot cutoff.
